ParlaMint-DK_20161013100445	That is, of course, just an important point. So I assumed somebody was gonna say something. But next week the European Union will have to decide on a temporary trade agreement, a temporary start to an economic and trade agreement with Canada, which is also called CETA. As far as is apparent, the Danish Government will vote in favour of this interim start-up. In order to prevent this, we have taken the extraordinary step of bringing the issue up in this Chamber. Our proposal is to and ours in this context means the Enhedslisten and the Alternativet's that in a vote shortly, the Danish Parliament shall require the government to vote against the European Commission's proposal for the provisional application of the agreement. It happens for two reasons. Firstly, because it is still unclear at the present time which elements will be put into effect temporarily, and that is a relatively central issue. The content of CETA can be divided into three main elements: a part that has already been handed over as EU competence and where national parliaments are completely outside influence; a part which is mixed competence and which also needs to be approved by national parliaments; and, finally, a part which is purely national competence and which therefore has to be decided only in individual countries. If the temporary entry into force only covers the part that is already the exclusive competence of the European Union, then it could be understood, but we have no guarantee of that. As the Foreign Minister himself writes in a reply to question 8, and I quote: "As I explained at the meeting of the European Committee of the Regions on 7 October 2016, it is not definitively decided which parts of CETA will be put into temporary application. And that's an answer that's come a few days ago. Apparently, the Treaties are intended to enter into force in a number of areas where shared powers exist and where approval by national parliaments should otherwise be a prerequisite. This is true, for example. Chapter 22 on trade and sustainable development, Chapter 23 on trade and the labour market, and Chapter 24 on trade and the environment. Therefore, I would also like to ask the Minister to confirm here from Parliament's platform that if our proposal should be voted down against common sense, of course, this will mean that this agreement will enter into force in a number of areas which otherwise normally require national parliamentary approval. The second reason why we are taking this step is that we are going to protest against the whole way in which the government has dealt with this matter. In Denmark we have handed over a lot of legislative power, legislative competence, from the Danish Parliament to the European Union. To compensate for this, we have made an agreement that, prior to negotiations and votes of greater scope for Denmark, the government is seeking a mandate in the Parliamentary Committee on Europe, thus ensuring a kind of parliamentary control over development. However, in this case of CETA, which started back in 2009, the government has never at any time applied to the Parliamentary European Committee for a negotiating mandate. Is it, then, because CETA is not of greater scope than the fact that it does not really matter whether or not CETA is adopted? It's completely ridiculous. This is an agreement of enormous importance, and I would also like to ask the Minister to confirm this, namely that it is an agreement with an enormous significance which the government is very keen to see finally adopted when the time comes. In conclusion, this case raises some very important questions in terms of parliamentary control of EU policy in Denmark. If we just let it go through, what are the consequences for the future? Then far-reaching agreements with enormous implications for employees' rights, for the environment and for food safety can be negotiated without any parliamentary mandate ever being given. When the result is available, it will only be set in motion on a temporary basis without parliamentary approval, and then we will be able to decide a long time later in the Folketing whether we will approve an already functioning agreement or whether we will at that time abandon all the problems that it will then give in that situation. In our view, such a development is not acceptable. We know that there are several parties that have clearly explained this. We also know that rapporteurs from parties, who I believe will be voting in favour of today, have clearly said that this was not an acceptable solution, and we therefore look forward to the debate and look forward to an explanation of why there might be a majority in order to put this agreement into effect temporarily. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013101009	Thank you. A number of rapporteurs have joined us. It would be a good thing if everyone who wants to get on the platform would join us. First, it's sir. Peter Hummelgaard Thomsen, Social Democratic Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013101034	Thank you for the floor, President. Today we are debating a resolution tabled by the Enhedslisten and the Alternative to reject the provisional application of the trade agreement between the European Union and Canada. We therefore welcome the debate and I would like to stress that it is always important that we discuss both the advantages and disadvantages of agreements with other states. This debate is an opportunity for that. Let me start by emphasising that in the Social Democratic Party we clearly believe that it is in Denmark's interest that we can trade freely and easily with other countries, and we are also in favour of a trade agreement with Canada. There are several reasons for this. Firstly, a free trade agreement will make it easier and cheaper for European competitive companies to increase exports across the Atlantic. This means more jobs in Europe and in Denmark of high quality and good pay for the benefit of citizens. We feed on our exports. In 2015, Denmark's exports of goods and services amounted to approx. 7.6 billion kr. to Canada. It can improve and we see an even greater potential by trading with a country such as Canada, which on so many crucial points is similar to Europe. Secondly, more trade creates growth and prosperity. It contributes decisively to the financing of our welfare societies. As a small country and a small economy, Denmark has always benefited from being open to the outside world and trading with other countries. When competition tightens up, we adapt and become more skilled. Thirdly, we are pleased, and indeed confident, that the CETA chapter on trade and the labour market recognises the value of a coherent policy that can create decent work, a high level of worker protection combined with effective enforcement. The EU and Canada also recognise the importance of social dialogue and undertake to promote such dialogue. In the agreement, the EU and Canada recognise each other's right to lay down labour market rules. Laws and practices in the labour market shall ensure the protection of fundamental principles and rights at work, including the right to organise and to hold collective bargaining, the abolition of all forms of forced labour, the effective abolition of child labour and the elimination of discrimination in employment and employment. Similarly, labour law must comply with the 2008 ILO Declaration on Social Justice with a view to fair globalisation. Safety and health at work, minimum conditions of employment for employees, non-discrimination as regards working conditions, including for migrant workers. Finally, the EU and Canada recognise that it is inappropriate to promote trade or investment through a reduction or relaxation of protection levels in their legislation and labour market rules. Fourthly, we do not see that the agreement opens up a barn door for either privatisations or outbiddings. Both the EU and Canada have made a large number of reservations in this regard. This applies to education, health care, social services, water recovery, purification and distribution, and for this the European Commission has announced that the CETA Agreement fully protects public services. EU Member States will be able to operate public monopolies if they so wish, and the CETA agreement will not force or encourage governments to privatise or deregulate public services. The Member States will therefore continue to be able to decide which services they will maintain as universal and public and provide support to them if they so wish. The Commission also stresses that CETA will not prevent a government from redeploying an earlier decision to privatise different sectors. Finally, we also see a clear improvement in the provisions on investment protection compared to what has been the subject of the discussion on a trade agreement with the United States. Transparency of the process, appointed judges and appeal options are clear improvements. Firstly, as regards the substance of the resolution, it is quite common practice for mixed agreements to be provisionally applied during the ratification process. Secondly, one of the more discussed elements of the agreement, namely the investment protection element, is exempt from temporary application. Thirdly, the provisional application cannot take place until it has been approved by the elected representatives of Europe in this House, thereby guaranteeing democratic legitimacy. All in all, we believe that both Canada and the European Union will benefit from acting more easily and freer with each other. We believe that it can create better opportunities to create jobs and prosperity, and that does not mean that we do not understand those who are concerned. We do, but we believe that this agreement takes account of the main challenges, and that is why we cannot support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013103841	There's a ghost going through Europe. There's a ghost going through North America. There's a ghost going through large parts of the Western world. And as our Prime Minister said in the opening speech: if we build walls around ourselves to keep others out, we will lock ourselves up and we will not get richer. It is possible that not everyone is aware of it or wants to be, but the debate we have here today on CETA is therefore part of the same protectionist ghost, it is part of the same movement. Historically, we know that free trade is good. Free trade creates growth, free trade creates jobs, free trade helps to combat poverty, and yes, free trade also helps to bring peace to the world. We are not talking about complete free trade; we are talking about regulated free trade between legal societies, which have rules on environmental protection, workers' rights, etc. But we are talking about free trade, and free trade brings growth. I agree that, for example, in the demonstration of the nice young people we met on the stairs this morning, who hailed the agreement and praised us for entering into it and applauded and said thank you and so on. And threw confetti over us in jubilation that we now take this stand was mixed up with some irony. You have to be careful about that, because there could be less rapid members of parliament who took what they said at face value. But I think there was a little irony involved and that they really meant the opposite of what they were saying. I agree that those who stood on the stairs this morning are probably genuinely concerned and do not see themselves as part of some protectionist movement. So for the sake of them, I would like to say that there is no reason to be concerned. We are talking about two highly civilised societies, namely the countries of the European Union and Canada, and there is a clear common understanding between Canada and the EU countries that CETA will not lead to any deterioration in food safety, health, the environment or labour rights, because in future the European Union will retain the right to determine our own level of protection just as it has been in the past. Changes in the level of protection of the European Union require the same legislative procedure as is known today, and an international agreement with Canada cannot change that. It is clear that in the EU and Canada there are different approaches to a number of issues. What is the reason, of course, is one can only guess, but the EU's red lines are fixed, and CETA will not change, for example, the EU's rules on the use of GMOs, in food, or, for that matter, the use of hormones in meat production. We look at the two issues differently. I don't know if it's gonna be like that. Canada may change its point of view; it may be that we do, but we look differently in those areas and that does not change CETA. The precautionary principle of the European Union is also in place. But then we have to look at those areas where there are common standards and comparable levels of protection, but where the rules are slightly different. Here there is the possibility of removing technical barriers to trade, removing trade barriers without affecting the level of protection. This will benefit both companies and consumers. So it is only the unnecessary regulation and the more bureaucratic barriers that we need to overcome. We must not put an end to the regulation based on real differences in the level of protection. CETA is the result of a long and tough negotiation process between EU Member States and Canada. It is an agreement aimed at increasing market access between the two markets, removing or lowering tariff barriers for the benefit of trade and, above all, harmonising approval systems in a number of areas. I know that the Enhedslisten looks at these issues, not least the issue of free trade, in a different way than we do. It is good that we are two different parties, a party rooted in communism and another party rooted in liberalism. That's the way it is, and I have full respect for that. But sometimes, when you hear the thunder from the left, it is as if Armageddon is waiting just around the corner, and the environment and workers' rights and democracy, and what I know will go the whistle if we enter into a trade agreement. One might then wonder, when we have entered into similar agreements with Vietnam, with South Korea, with Colombia, that we have not met the criticism, in spite of the fact that these are commercial cultures, about working cultures which are considerably further away from those we have and those of the Canadians. I think that's worth thinking about. Because there is no indication that this trade agreement will have the major negative consequences that the proposers point to. On the contrary, this is a world-class free trade agreement. CETA will set the standard for an entirely new generation of free trade agreements, which also reduce economic growth and sustainability. At the same time, the agreement will also bring great benefits to Denmark: we will be able to look forward to an extra export of goods and services of up to 2.2 billion. kr. annual. So, in other words, there are quite a lot of good arguments for concluding free trade agreements such as CETA. It is both an economic but indeed a political investment to get closer to our friends around the world. Then there is the purely procedural argument on which you hang your hat. I might see that somewhere in theory there may be something in it. In other words, can we really approve an agreement which has not yet been finalised in all the Member States? However, it is the case, and has been the case for many years, that in practice the temporary application of a free trade agreement is allowed until it has been finalised. Why do you do that? This can be explained by the fact that, unfortunately, for some Member States, this will take a very long time to complete an agreement. For example, it took four years from the signing of the agreement between the EU and South Korea until it had been finalised in all EU Member States. Then it is also worth bearing in mind that the provisional application takes place only in those areas in which the European Union has competence, that is to say, either exclusive or shared competence. And in the areas of shared competence, the Council of Ministers, with which the Danish Government is involved, must also be involved. It is a political choice. So, in other words, a full and full trade agreement will not be opened until ratification is in place in all the Member States. And then there is the democratic element in the European Parliament, where we have virtually all representatives. So when the government is going to celebrate that we are writing this during October 18th, I personally intend to participate in the celebration as well. I want to go to a restaurant on October 18th, and I want to have dinner with my family, and it's gonna be a really, really good restaurant. I'm not gonna tell you what kind of restaurant this is gonna be, because it's gonna be a secret to my wife, Jane, who happens to be 50 years old the same day.

At least that's what we're trying to do. A few direct questions were asked by Mr. Søren Søndergaard, but first of all: Thank you for the debate. I would also like to say at the outset that it is indeed gratifying, as I have noted at least, that there still seems to be broad support among the parliamentary parties for free trade. After all, exports represent more than half of our GDP in Denmark, and thus trade with other nations is absolutely necessary for our country's prosperity and job creation now and also in the future. The free trade agenda is, perhaps some will say under attack, and others will say challenged, problematized, and that is, in effect, regrettable. This is certainly the government's starting point, because it is the case that 31 million jobs in the EU are directly dependent on trade with other countries outside the EU, and this is the case with a very large number of jobs in Denmark. And it is not the government's view that it will benefit those jobs and future job creation if we raise barriers to trade in the future. On the contrary, it is the government's view that we should continue to seek more free trade, not less. Then it has been asked why the Folketing has not been involved. The answer to that is that the government has presented CETA to the European Parliamentary Committee on a number of occasions • I think it is nine times that I have been informed • since 2009 when changing governments and changing foreign ministers have informed about the negotiating directives from the Commission on the start of negotiations with Canada. There has then been ample opportunity under successive governments for the parties to make their views known. It is also the impression of the government that, whenever the state of the negotiations has been presented, there has been broad support for the agreement with Canada and, moreover, for the EU to continue to expand its network of free trade agreements with third countries. It is, of course, fair to choose to take a different view here and now, but there have been opportunities for the Folketing to have at least raised a flag and said: we need a more detailed discussion of this. This could have been one of the nine times that the case has been referred to. Then things develop, and that is fair enough, but then we must also bear in mind that the government's assessment of a case is, of course, evolving. There must be parallelism in things like this. Then there has been a concern about the level of protection. I have to say here that it is not the government's view that the agreement leads to a lower level of protection in Denmark and the European Union. On the contrary, CETA is precisely about establishing a high standard of trade on equal terms, and that is why the agreement not only contains provisions on tariffs and regulatory cooperation to promote trade, but also contains ambitious chapters on sustainable development, labour rights and the environment. And then, as Mrs Christina Egelund also mentioned, the basic point of departure is that Canada is not one of the countries which traditionally raises the greatest concerns about environmental or worker considerations. There are other countries where that concern may be more justified than in relation to Canada, but it is of course a fair concern to travel from the time of the Folketing. But it is a concern about something that, in the view of the government, is addressed in the agreement. Then there is the question of this temporary application and whether it is something completely new. It is also fair to raise it from the side of a number of parties and from those who tabled the resolution, i.e. the Enhedslisten and the Alternative. And if some people today in this Chamber and during the previous reading of the proposal problematize the specific question of temporary application, it is the government's view that it must at least be based on a misunderstanding, if you think it is something new. It is fair to criticise it, but it is nothing new. Temporary application is quite common practice and has also been the case with some of the other free trade agreements concluded by the EU and Denmark via the EU. It might also be asked whether anyone will be heard before the provisional application begins. Jan E. Jørgensen, my fellow party member, mentioned it. Actually, there is. A democratic parliamentary guard has been set up to ensure that not only are we going into temporary use, but that it is being verified, and that it is doing so, among other things. by the EU Member States, which first have to give the green light, and then the European Parliament must approve the agreement before the relevant parts are put into temporary use. In the view of the government, this at least ensures broad democratic support for putting into effect those parts which are within the competence of the European Union and which the Council of Ministers agrees should not wait for the often unfortunately lengthy national ratification processes. After all, it is not the Folketing that is referred to here, but the parliaments of other countries where there are somewhat more lengthy ratification processes. At the same time, I would also like to point out and point out that investment protection, including the new dispute settlement system, ICS, is not part of the temporary application. That is why it is the government's view that we can politically problematize these things that we may politically disagree with the government, but as such the matter is unproblematic and we therefore want the Folketing to reject the resolution. Then there is the question of why the government will later apply for a paragraph 19 assent for ratification. In other words, when the government announces that it intends later to obtain the consent of the Folketing for the ratification of CETA under Section 19 of the Constitution, this is due to the fact that the agreement, both in the Folketing and in the general public as well as in other EU countries, has been of particular interest in recent times. And that is, of course, the kind of government prerogatives that I think Mr. Jan E. Jørgensen was talking about finding out when and if there is such legal justification for it, one should of course follow the legal assessment • It is politically wise to seek support in the Parliamentary Committee on Europe or in the Folketing. After all, there is always a balance in line with what political signals are given, what signals are from the public and what international debate there is about these things. But the government does not want to leave any doubt that the Folketing can clearly express its position on Denmark's ratification of the agreement, and therefore there will be this exercise in spring 2017 regarding it. Then there were two specific questions for me from Søren Søndergaard. One is that Søren Søndergaard asks me to confirm that this is a large and comprehensive agreement, and there is. It is an agreement that will have a significant impact on Denmark and, hopefully, a significant positive impact on job creation and Danish exports. So, yes, I can confirm that. Then there is the question of the division of powers, in other words what is specifically proposed for temporary application. It is suggested that most of the agreement will be provisionally applied, due to the fact that the vast majority of the agreement falls within the scope of the EU's common commercial policy. However, certain parts will be exempted. It is about portfolio investment and investment protection and dispute settlement. I mentioned this before, ICS and criminal law measures at camcording, which is certainly an English word, which I think, if I have to translate into Danish, what the rules prescribe, means that as a guest in a cinema you are not allowed to record the film. So Søren Søndergaard's second question is no.

Denmark's climate policy needs to play along with the European and global agenda and be really emission-reducing in a global perspective. Denmark is a small and open economy in a large international market and we alone cannot change the climate. There are many others and much more that affect global climate fluctuations. That is why, in the Liberal Alliance, we do not believe that Denmark should pursue any global major power policy in the area of climate change without really having the muscles to do so. During the phase of the so-called green transition, Denmark has been outbursting for many years in the first stages. It has wasted its strength, and it has also cost economic growth, bearing in mind that economic growth is designed to provide the basis for technological development, which is the solution to the challenge, by creating a low-carbon society in the future. We've got some attention and TV time for our taxpayers' money. It is, of course, gratifying if the many Danish support crowns have helped to pave the way for the big countries, those with the big muscles and the very large CO2 emissions, to have seen alternatives to coal and oil and now to take part of the responsibility on them • and perhaps even buy Danish-produced solutions in the future. But now the tactics have to be changed. We should not increase the head start to the main field anymore, because it will create further distortions of competition and cost further economic growth and more jobs in Denmark. We need to save our energy and get our breath back, and perhaps we need to get to the front of the main field and get to power and get ready for the mass spruce.But unlike when Lars Bak pulls the Tour de France field into Paris, we should all end up together standing at the top of the podium in both 2030 and 2050 and even further out in the future. So all in all we in the Liberal Alliance welcome the fact that the ratification of the agreement is progressing and we support the fact that Denmark, with our ratification, can now contribute further to the progress of the process. Thank you for the floor.

Denmark must push ahead with this process. We know that conversion is technological, physical, biological and chemical possible and that it will only be more expensive the longer we wait to set things in motion. That's why we want a stop to the sale of fossil cars by 2025. That is why we want a sustainable conversion of agriculture, where climate pressures are minimized and production is much less animal than it is today. That is why we want a green tax reform where it will be more expensive to pollute and cheaper to make it climate-friendly. And that is why we will soon be tabling a motion for a resolution that all new government legislation should be assessed in relation to the consequences for the objectives of the Paris Agreement and the 17 new world objectives for sustainable development. If I am right and there is really a climate crisis, but we are not doing anything, we are in fact in danger of creating a holocaust for our children and grandchildren. If you climate sceptics are right and there is no climate crisis, but we are doing what we can to live up to the Paris Agreement, then in 30 years' time we have created a better world with clean air, clean water, healthy children, green jobs, energy independence, even if there was no climate crisis to solve. I know what I prefer. Denmark plays a crucial role in the global transition to renewable energy. We can be those who show the rest of the world that it is possible, as we have done before, that that is what creates jobs and that is what can create welfare. The world needs an example of a serious sustainable transformation with a fundamental revolution of the way we organise ourselves. ParlaMint-DK_20161013144601	Thank you. The proposal we are debating, L 31, is to freeze the subsidy to the Faroe Islands at a nominal value of 641.8 million. kr. in the years from 2016 up to and including 2019. This is the result of both a negotiation between the coalition parties in the Faroe Islands and then negotiation between the Faroese Government and the Ministry of Finance in Copenhagen. It is in the interests of most of the Faroe Islands that we strengthen our economy so that our welfare is fully paid with our own money. It animates a responsible economic policy both in the short and in the long term, and it reduces the risk that the economy is distorted by revenue without any productive substance. The freezing of the block grant is not only a step in the right direction, but also quite natural, bearing in mind that our average value creation is in line with the Danish one and may soon exceed it. Every country should endeavour to eliminate dependence and external subsidies and create a self-sustaining economy. In the Faroe Islands we are approaching this reality as the Faroese taxpayers are now paying close to 90 per cent. of the Faroese Finance Act and the block grant amount to just over 10%. In Danish terms, however, this means that the grant to the Faroe Islands is less than one thousandth of the Danish Finance Act. I think that we in the Faroe Islands should speed up the reduction of the block subsidy. However, as I said, there must also be a political consensus from home. However, I also believe that Denmark can actively contribute to a faster transition to a fully sustainable economy in the Faroe Islands if a more pragmatic approach to foreign policy and trade policy is taken. Here it is a question of loosening the constraints of foreign policy law and creating the best conditions for access to markets and resources, so that we can continue to keep the Faroe Islands competitive in relation to our competitors. We have recently had two significant examples of Denmark having delegated trade policy mandates to the EU to conclude agreements that seem to strengthen Danish industry and value creation, but leave the Faroe Islands and no doubt Greenland in a weakened competitive situation in terms of trade with Denmark and with the EU. One is about the free trade agreement with Canada, which we have discussed this morning, and the other is about the aviation agreement between the European Union and the United States. In the Canadian context, Canadians are now offering better access to exports of fish to Denmark and the EU than the Faroe Islands have. And in the mandate to the aviation agreement, the Faroe Islands and Greenland have been excluded from the agreement, which has been left to the EU to negotiate. However, the Danish side has instructed dealers to prepare the way for later inclusion of Norway and Iceland, but not the Faroe Islands and Greenland. Until trade policy chains have been loosened, Denmark should at least make sure that the other parts of the so-called Kingdom community are not made worse when Denmark enters into agreements. It should also be in Denmark's interest for the Faroe Islands to have the necessary opportunities to develop a strong economy, for the sole reason that Denmark benefits from the Faroese purchasing power through a trade surplus of 1.2 billion. kr. in respect of the Faroe Islands, in particular: based on the fact that Denmark and the EU have a full free trade agreement with the Faroe Islands, while the Faroe Islands have a limited trade agreement with Denmark and the EU. So just to the remark made by Rasmus Jarlov that the 642 million. DKK, which Denmark pays to the Faroe Islands in grants, is not a large amount: I agree that it is a relatively small amount in a Faroese context, but it is actually quite significant in a Danish context at the nearest pepper nuts, because it is less than 1 thousand. It is certainly inexpensive that sovereignty over a country with 300,000 square kilometres of land and sea area has been taken over for such a small amount of money. Moreover, I shall report that I shall probably be voting in favour of the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013151317	Thank you for that, President. The draft law here is the implementation of two political agreements concluded in the spring, on the one hand, the agreement of 18 March to strengthen the quality of the free primary schools and, on the other hand, the sub-agreement of 10 May, which forms part of the overall package on radicalisation and religious preachers, etc. The Social Democratic Party has signed the first agreement and is a party to the second agreement and therefore, of course, supports this bill. We believe that there is a need to strengthen the impartiality of general supervision of private and free schools. That is why we welcome the stricter requirements that will apply in the future in relation to whom the parent company of a given free school can choose as a supervisor. We also think that openness and transparency about the financial donations that a free school may receive are important. The new rules ensure that the supervisor and everyone else are easily aware of any donations, including donations of more than DKK 20,000. and the specific information on each donation. In addition, the enhanced supervision will be strengthened, i.e. the supervision that the Ministry can implement, if there is doubt, on the basis of general supervision, about whether a given free school meets the requirements of the Free School Act. In these cases, in future, the Ministry will participate as an observer at the school's board meetings, as well as supervisory practices will be changed in order to expand the possibilities of conducting interviews with the students and the use of unannounced supervision visits. Finally, it is now explicitly written into the Free School Act that free schools should develop and strengthen the democratic education of pupils. I think it is important to emphasise, as the comments and consultation notes also show, that the intention is not to restrict the freedom of free schools to run schools on a certain attitude-based basis. Democratic education is not, of course, about the fact that one should mean something quite specific in all kinds of areas or that one should have a certain pedagogy. On the contrary, democratic education is about respecting freedom of diversity, freedom of spirit, whether you like it or not, and fighting not only for your own freedom but also for the freedom of the others, across political, religious and all sorts of other differences. The free schools will still have the freedom to run school on an attitude-based basis, and as the comments also state, also to seek to influence their pupils towards this basis. However, it is clarified and made clear that it must be done in a way that respects those who have different attitudes and views of life than oneself and, in general, respect for the diversity of views and beliefs that characterise a democratic society. Democratic education is, in many ways, a prerequisite for the Danish tradition of freedom which free schools themselves enjoy, and we should all be involved in safeguarding that tradition of freedom, and so I see that far, by far, most free schools do. With this amendment, we are making it even more clear that we expect free schools to contribute to democratic education and, at the same time, we are strengthening the supervision that this is actually what is happening in the various free and private schools. As I said, social democracy supports this proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013151929	Thank you. The bill is a implementation of the agreement of 10 May 2016 on free schools, which forms part of a comprehensive package to deal with those parts of the Muslim environments that seek to undermine Danish laws and values and which support parallel legal perceptions and parallel legal systems in Denmark. The parties behind the agreement agree that state supervision of free primary schools should be further strengthened. After all, it was intended to be strengthened, but now there is something extra on top. It is suggested that the law lays down that the free primary schools should, according to their purpose and throughout their activities, strengthen the democratic education of pupils and, at the very least, prepare them to live in the Danish people's government. It will be possible for the Ministry, as an observer in the context of enhanced supervision, to participate in the school's board meetings, general meetings and parent meetings. It will also be possible to go in and talk to the students without the presence of teachers or staff at the school. We think that is an excellent step to take. Of course, this may also go a little further, and that is why we also think that there is a good balance in the legislative proposal, in which it is suggested that parents can, of course, refuse their children to engage in such a conversation, because in the end the children are the parents'. But it can also be part of an overall assessment if all or very many parents refuse to allow their children to participate in a conversation with a representative from the board or ministry. Then it may be that there is something behind it or behind it that can form the basis for digging down an extra layer to see what is going on at that school. So there will be better supervision, there will also be more staff to the board, there will be a limitation on the period of supervision, and there will be more openness on the donations to private schools so that the authorities and the public can better monitor who is involved in funding schools. It is no secret that it is something that we think has been significant on the part of the Danish People's Party. We also know that there is work going on in the central administration and the government in order to see whether there can be even more openness in other areas and whether, in some cases, foreign donations can be banned, that is. prevent them from entering Denmark. It is a work which we will follow closely and which we find very interesting. With these comments, the Danish People's Party can support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013153220	Thank you. I would like to start by saying that I think it is remarkable when reading the draft law, how weak a link there is between the proposal, Rambøll's evaluation of supervisory arrangements for the free schools from 2015, and then the political agreement, which is the starting point for the whole legislation. Although the legislative notes refer to the report, it is in fact given the impression that the evaluation of supervision has played a very modest role, because the key elements of the draft law appear completely unrelated to what can be read in the evaluation report. The lack of coherence is a contributing factor in Enhedslisten's concern about the overall legislative proposal. In general terms, it is incredibly important that the Folketing does not legislate on the basis of sudden political moods and that, in the specific case, we do not change the essential basic conditions for free schools without a proper analysis of the conditions that are desired to change. And our concern has not diminished by the consultation responses received. This does not mean that we do not find there are positive elements in the draft law. That is, for example, the case. We welcome the fact that the issue of impartiality in supervision is being addressed, and we think it is an excellent proposal that the circle of people who cannot be elected as supervisors should be expanded and that both the individual and the entire parliamentary term should be shortened. On the other hand, we think that we need to look at the proposal for a new framework for talks with students. A number of the consultation responses express a criticism of the proposed scheme, which we think should give rise to adjustments in the legislative proposal. We do not at first disagree with the fact that, as part of supervision, there must be an opportunity to hold talks with pupils without the presence of school leaders and teachers. We have also noted that it will be accepted if parents oppose the participation of their children, but we believe that the legal notes contain a barely wrapped threat of consequences if parents avail themselves of this right. We find this extremely inconvenient. Rather than the distrust expressed to parents in the legislative notes, we believe that the law should instead make it clear that parents, as a rule, always participate if supervision talks are carried out with individual pupils in exceptional cases. We also think that, in the light of the many responses to the consultation, it is necessary to clarify what the proposal for the registration of the concept of democratic education actually implies for the practice of schools. It is obvious that there will be no one in this Chamber who believes that free schools should not be based on a basis of value that respects the fundamental democratic values of our societies. Nor do I think there are any parties in the Folketing who would question whether the free school world associations profess democratic values. And yet it should be noted that these associations question the way in which the concept of democratic education is proposed to be incorporated into the law. This is done from the Danish Free School Association, from the Danish Private School Association, from the Danish Free School Teacher Association, from Grundtvigsk Forum and several others. I therefore think it is important that in today's debate and in the further legislative process we have a very careful look at what is the substance of the criticism raised in the hearings. It is called for what the registration of the concept of democratic education implies in relation to the teaching and overall operation of schools. What is the significance of incorporating the concept, when it is already stated in the law, that schools should, throughout their activities, prepare pupils to live in a society such as Danish, with freedom and popular rule, and develop and strengthen students' knowledge and respect of fundamental freedom and human rights? Does this change anything? Is the addition an expression of the fact that we are merely mentioning the democratic basis for the functioning of the free schools once more, or is it a strengthening of the requirements of schools? What exactly is the tightening up, and what are the consequences for the content of supervision? I think we need answers to that. The draft law, which has been consulted, said, for example: on democratic education, that it is not enough if the school confines itself to preparing pupils for having to settle for living in a free democracy. In order to fulfil this provision, the school must, in words and deeds, pave the way for pupils to take the values of free democratic society into account. I have noticed that those sentences have disappeared again in the final legislative proposal, and I would like to hear: is it because the Minister agrees with Grundtvigsk Forum that the wording was clearly restricting freedom of spirit in relation to free schools? Or are there other reasons why these wordings have been regretted during the process? I would also like to ask the Minister to clarify in greater detail what lies in the legislative texts on the clarification and effective enforcement of the school's decorum requirements, which I think are unclear. The Enhedslisten cannot support the bill as it stands here. We hope that in the further legislative process we will be able to clarify the concrete consequences of the bill and that the government will be open to drafting the bill so that it responds to a number of the critical comments that have come from the free school world. I look forward to further work on the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013154448	Thank you to the rapporteur. No one else has asked for brief comments. The next rapporteur for Mrs Merete Riisager from the Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013154455	The free schools are the heartblood of the Liberal Alliance. We in Denmark, as citizens, have a constitutional right to open a school on our own initiative, and this gives us a great opportunity to offer our children a different education from that offered by the state. This has given us a basic education system in which the private and the public can inspire each other, and it gives parents a free choice of schools that goes far beyond the free choice of parents, for example in the area of day care and youth education. The free schools are a gift, and we have to watch them. That is why Liberal Alliance is opposed to virtually any proposal to restrict the right of free schools to act as independent and parent-controlled entities. Free schools must remain free. And they must comply only with two general requirements. One is what we call a high standard of professional content, and the other is what politicians call the democratic requirement, that is, that schools should provide schools on a democratic basis. The State should not interfere with which pupils attend a school and how schools organize their education and school day. That is precisely what is the strength of free schools. On the other hand, we are firm that these two requirements must be met. Free schools, whose professionalism does not meet the expectations that one can have, must lose their subsidies, and the same applies to schools which do not provide schools on a democratic basis. You have the right to keep school, but you have no right to be approved as such and receive state aid if the school's basis is not based on the basic pillars of democracy. This is, of course, to ensure that each child is taught a lesson that enables him or her to function in a democracy, and it is also to protect the freedoms which form the pillars of democracy. A State is not obliged to support institutions or organisations working to undermine the values and rights that guarantee the freedom of the individual. That is why we want schools which, for example, place religious laws above worldly ones, which do not recognise gender equality and equal rights in society, and whose values do not recognise the freedoms that guarantee individual freedom and civil society rights, to lose their subsidies. This does not mean that at a school, for example, there cannot be a teacher who has views that stick out, but that the school in its value base and function shows that freedoms are not recognised as having priority. I attended a conference organized by the free schools on this subject a few days ago. There seems to be a concern in school circles as to whether free schools should change their practice in the light of this change of law. The short answer is that you should not. Free schools constitute the DNA of democracy itself, and the law here is expected to affect a very, very small proportion of free schools. The concern is and I would say quite frankly, because it is no use pretending that some of the Muslim free schools do not comply with the democratic demand. Schools have the right to set up a school based on their beliefs, but the law does not apply if, for example, the school puts Sharia law above the constitution, if the girls meet with special dress requirements, if atheism is not accepted, if Islam criticism is not allowed at school or if apostates from Islam are not accepted. Whether it is indeed the case that some schools do not function on a democratic basis is up to the Ministry to clarify, and it is of course crucial that the employees who work in this area are people who have a deep knowledge of Danish free school tradition, democratic education and also Islam and the various interpretations of it. The challenge will be to close down and it will not be easy for the grant to those schools that do not meet the democratic requirement, while at the same time not bothering everyone else. This is an important task that I expect the Ministry to do in the very best and most professional way.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013155336	Thank you. So if there's anything new in this bill, it's the language. I think we can all agree on that, and I think that is what has led to the great concerns of the free school world, because there are some new concepts in play and there are some new concepts in games that are not being defined very well. Democratic formation. What is it? The demand for freedom and the rule of the people? That is already in the current legislation. This means that schools must, according to their own purpose and throughout their activities, prepare pupils to live in a society of freedom and democracy, and that they must develop and strengthen pupils' knowledge and respect for fundamental freedoms and human rights. In the law on post-schools and free vocational schools, the concept of democratic education is used. It says here that it is about maintaining and developing a process leading towards democracy, and it is the school's task to form the pupils of committed citizens with the desire and ability to be active in a democratic society. We can easily subscribe to that in the alternative. I shall be the first to emphasise this. However, the comments on this draft law state that a change in the purpose clause is proposed, so that it is explicitly stated that the free primary schools must strengthen the democratic education of pupils as a whole in their aims and activities. So from the formation of pupils into committed citizens with the desire and ability to be active in a democratic society, their democratic formation must now be strengthened. There's a difference. It may well be that one may disagree on whether the interpretations are in that difference, but it is our claim that there is a difference in language. This concerns us in the alternative, and I think that is also what worries the free school world. It's not a religious free school, it's a creative drama and music school. I'm pretty sure the vast majority of parents would say: Nope, I don't want to be a part of that. Would this then mean that my son's free school in Østerbro should have withdrawn his grant because the vast majority of parents would say no to it? And would it make a difference if there was a Muslim free school that said the same thing? These are assumptions, and they're speculations, but I'm worried that may be the consequence of this bill. ParlaMint-DK_20161013162423	It was the quick speech of the Conservative rapporteur who just created a moment of inattentiveness! Thank you for your comments, and thank you for the great support that Parliament's parties also show for this bill. We have a long school tradition in Denmark with a strong focus on the democratic participation of children and young people. This applies not only in our primary school, but also in our free primary schools. Almost one fifth of the country's primary school pupils attend a free primary school, and the free primary schools have an important role to play in our society and in the development of both the democratic education of a very large group of our children and young people who receive their primary education in the free primary schools. But also in our society as a whole, among others. for some of the reasons that a number of the rapporteurs have also mentioned up here from the platform today, they play an important role. They do so, both in relation to having a different view of things, that one can have a different educational approach, and because one also fills in in many places a void created if the local school closes, or in other ways one chooses voluntarily to organise and build up a free primary school. I think that the diversity of free primary schools is an important expression of the fact that freedom and popular rule are central values in Danish society. I would therefore also like to make it very clear at the outset that the aim of the legislative proposal we are debating today is in no way to restrict the freedoms of free primary schools. The draft law does not interfere with the right of free primary schools to have a particular ideological or religious basis of value. It does not interfere with the right to be able to build an alternative school form. Having said that, it is also the case that free primary schools do not have a constitutional right to state subsidies, and that is perhaps why I think that today's debate is running a bit off the rails for certain parties, as it were, when one equates having freedom and having the right to receive state subsidies. The state can and also has the right to impose conditions on schools in order to receive a state grant under the existing primary school law. We have a match with the demand, we have freedom and the demands of the people. We can demand that school education, as well as schools, should contribute to the development of the democratic education of pupils as a whole. That is, in fact, the basis of the draft law. It is to extend and extend the dimension of freedom and popular rule, and it is to ensure that the tradition of education is also based on the free primary schools on a democratic basis, as fortunately it does in the free primary schools, and as a starting point for the vast majority. The bill we are discussing today translates the agreement reached on 18 March 2016 between the government, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservative People's Party on strengthening the quality of the free primary schools. It is an agreement which was later signed by the Socialists, the Socialist People's Party and the Radical Left, and in this context it also expanded both in scope and, above all, in resource capacity. I also think it sends out a signal that the legislative proposal we are debating today is not only based on thorough preparatory work, it is also broadly politically anchored. The discussions prior to the conclusion of the agreement included, inter alia: Based on Rambøll's report, the evaluation of supervisory arrangements in the free basic schools, from March 2015, and the report points out among other things: the need to strengthen the impartiality of the supervision of free primary schools. The draft law therefore also contains a number of legislative amendments which are necessary in order to also implement the sub-agreement subsequently concluded on 10 May 2016 on the free primary schools. The sub-agreement is part of a comprehensive package designed to stem religious preachers who seek to undermine Danish laws and values and support parallel legal perceptions. The partial agreement of 10 May was concluded between the Government, the Social Democrats, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Socialist People's Party and the Conservative People's Party and subsequently joined by the Radical Left. The partial agreement builds on the agreement reached on 18 March on the strengthening of the quality of free primary schools and also further strengthens state supervision of free primary schools. I think that both agreements represent precisely the balance that must be struck; they guarantee freedom for free primary schools to have a different educational view, a different ideological or religious basic view, while emphasising that there must be both resources present and a stronger focus on those schools where there may be a need for stronger supervision. It is about the cohesion of Denmark, and it is precisely because we have a strong democracy, that we have our freedom and our folk rule, and here schools, whether we speak primary school or free primary schools, play a crucial role, because that is where a basis is laid not only for education but also for education. The draft law must therefore also help to counteract the tedious trend towards cultural parallel societies, which we unfortunately see not only in Denmark but also in countries around us. That is why I would also like to thank all the parties that have taken responsibility for the agreements, also taken responsibility for finding the resources for closer supervision. One thing is to have supervision on paper, but if there are no resources for supervision, then we do not, in fact, ensure that we take care of the interests of the pupils, that we carry out the desire, which we have broadly politically, to ensure that nothing is going on that is contrary to freedom and the rule of the people. The draft law also explicitly states that free primary schools must, according to their purpose and throughout their activities, strengthen the democratic education of pupils. It is already clear from today's free school law that, according to their purpose and throughout their activities, free primary schools are to prepare pupils to live in a society such as Danish with freedom and popular rule and to develop and strengthen pupils' knowledge and respect for fundamental freedoms and human rights, including gender equality. The proposed clarification on democratic education aims to ensure that all schools, throughout their activities, actively develop and strengthen the democratic education of all pupils. I think that we are also saying in this way that, of course, schools have both a right and an opportunity to legally influence pupils, but that must not destroy or harm the democratic education of pupils. This must be done in a way that contributes to the development of the democratic education of pupils and does not counteract it. One of the things that is also included in the broad political agreements is an indicator-based supervision, which now needs to be further developed with a focus on freedom and popular rule. I think that is also an expression of the fact that this is not a question of arbitrariness, as I hear some of the rapporteurs say a little bit. This isn't about reacting on the basis of individual cases. This is about strengthening the basic quality of the way we oversee free schools. At the same time, the agreement reached on 10 May also included, inter alia, the following: The Social Democrats, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Socialist People's Party and subsequently joined by the Radical Left also created a quantification of how many supervisory visits should then be made when a school has been appointed for enhanced supervision, and also emphasised that the unannounced inspection visit is a tool to be used to a greater extent, including that it should also be possible to include the students' perspective on the matter and to hold talks with pupils, without there being any leaders, without there being teachers, in order also to ensure the rights of the children and give them the right to speak with their own voice. The agreement also means that there must be and must be at least two unannounced inspection visits to the schools in which the board initiates an oversight case with the requirement of freedom and public governance on the basis of requests from parents, teachers and pupils. I think it is reassuring that we are also sending the signal of the agreement of 10 May that in future, if you are concerned as a former pupil or a parent about quality or about some of what is going on in a free school, you can also be comforted by the fact that those enquiries are taken seriously and that they are also followed up. We are also launching a number of other concrete initiatives that will help strengthen supervision and will help to ensure that, when we talk about financial supervision, we also ensure that schools are truly independent and that there is greater transparency in relation to who donations are made to schools. Therefore, in the future, there will also be a requirement, both in the financial statements and also in the supervisory statement, to provide information on donations over a certain amount. It is also to ensure that schools remain independent of people, other schools, courses, companies, institutions, countries and others. I also think that it is an important signal that we are sending out that, in such a way, there is a need to focus both on freedom and popular rule, democratic education and supervision, but also to focus on whether one can buy oneself to influence a school through one's donations. It is also emphasized, and this is something that has also emerged from some of the consultation answers that, of course, there is no question of restricting parents' ability to volunteer, not cleaning, repair work or other help to schools. It is not considered donations, and that is why I also think that the concern that sometimes sounds about the fact that people are now cutting into freedom and culture is extremely exaggerated in relation to the fact that there is a very precise distinction between what is involved in promoting the school culture that is strong and that we want to continue to be strong, and what we can have a concern about can help to break down a strong basic school structure and culture in Denmark. The free primary schools today are very different. They are based on different values, and they must continue to do so. It's part of their strength. But all schools must also comply with the law's requirement that the teaching should prepare the pupils for a life in a society such as the Danish with freedom and folk rule, and where there is a need for education, education and democratic education to go hand in hand. The government supports the free school tradition and we recognise that the vast majority of free schools are doing very well. And that is why they probably have no reason to fear stricter supervision if there is nothing to come for. The draft law, on the other hand, provides us with better tools for looking out and also for supervising those schools where there may be a concern that it is lagging behind, so that hopefully they can also get a positive development going, or that, where there is a need for what has happened through history, we can close schools or deprive them of subsidies. The Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats) and European Democrats welcomes the Commission's proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the Member States relating to the labelling, presentation and advertising of tobacco products. Why have you listened to what, among other things? Grundtvigsk Forum and some others say in their hearing answer? I think everything else would be weird. I think it would be arrogance without equal if we did not listen to some of the things that have been included in the hearings. This does not mean that we agree politically with all the consultation responses, but there will of course be elements in the consultation answers which give rise to clarifications in a legislative proposal and which hopefully all help to ensure that the legislative proposal becomes clearer and also easier to administer afterwards. That is why I would like to say thank you, both for the constructive and thorough discussions we have had with the parties of the Folketing, but also for the process that has been followed, in terms of qualifying how we ensure that the enhanced supervision of the free primary schools does not go beyond the culture and strong culture of freedom that we have in the free primary school area in Denmark and must continue to have. I look forward to the committee reading, and I therefore hope that the breadth of the work so far can continue, because it sends out a signal that this is not, as someone says, a turkey hunt for the few, but a well-prepared initiative to ensure the quality of our free primary schools in the future too.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013165214	Thank you. And that, in our opinion, is a really bad combination. When you go into legislation, there are already opportunities today for municipalities to enter into operating agreements with self-owned institutions which are affiliated to a company or have agreements for booking a number of places for children and employees from certain companies. It has existed in the last 30, 40, perhaps even more years. This is well known in the institutional world. The same possibility exists if the municipalities choose to outsource part of their day care facilities. So far, not a single one has been outsourced, so that is not a very practical problem, one can say, and if you want private institutions at last, then it is already in the legislation that private institutions can be set up which have the full power to decide who should be admitted. It may then perhaps be a decision from the institution concerned that it should be directed precisely at employees of a given company. So nothing really new is added. The only real difference is that, in future, the self-serving institutions must be able to exclude children whose parents are not employed in a given company from being admitted when there are empty places in the institution, that is to say, to add an opportunity for lower capacity utilisation in this area, and perhaps that is not real progress. I would also like to say that if you look at the possibility of setting up daily offers with English, German or French as the main language, then I quite share Mrs Pernille Bendixen's view that there is no further possibility here. On the contrary, we are restricting opportunities because we are shutting down the possibility of creating day-to-day offers with other main languages. So again: You don't open up new opportunities, you make further restrictions. In short, there is little evidence that the draft law can pave the way for real improvements in relation to existing opportunities in the two areas to which the law is aimed. On the other hand, there may be a significant risk, particularly with a focus on the opportunities that the bill points to to establish business-oriented international day offers, that it paves the way for more private day offers, where employees work under conditions that are not regulated by the agreements of the area and where there are no requirements for the employees' educational background. With the experience we have so far from day-to-day offers, which have been created primarily to receive children from foreign workers, experience is that it has paved the way for massive social dumping in this area and a weakening of educational quality, and that is simply not needed any more. It may be difficult to assess the extent of the problems posed by legislative changes. I believe and hope, in any case, that it will be incredibly limited what practical consequences this will have for the area at all, but I think we should go another way. It could be that we extended the opportunity that already exists today for the self-serving institutions to reserve seats for employees' children, that we should consider jointly following the recommendation of the children's and cultural leaders, which points out that, instead of going the private road, we could consider whether a space could be created for municipal institutions to reserve a number of places in the same way. It might also be very sensible for us to be more determined to set up either whole institutions or groups within the existing institutions which could receive children with a different language. If we did so, if we took advantage of the opportunities that exist in the public institutions, the municipal institutions, we would thus have some very good offers which had very flexible possibilities and which had it in them that it was certain that the conditions that apply to children and to employees fully complied with the quality requirements and contractual expectations that we have for our day-to-day offer area today. So I think it is quite clear that the Enhedslisten cannot support the proposal that has been put forward.

This applies, of course, if you are a nurse or if you are an industrial worker or if you are a servant, that you can have changing working hours, you can have night work, and therefore it is also important to say that this can apply both to public jobs and to private jobs, but where, when you are also part of being able to offer a job, for example. is with changing working hours, may be a family where there is also some logistics and some internship that needs to fall into place. And the fact that, as a company with this bill in the future, you have the opportunity to reserve some of the places for the children of employees of companies also makes sure that it is not a lack of care provision, for example. a late afternoon, an evening or the weekend, which means that an employee will not be able to accept a job. One of the things that has also been important, and which is also reflected in the bill, is that it applies to both the director's children and the porter's children in the hospital. It is part of this that it does not fundamentally break with our thinking that we should have institutions with equal access. What is also being proposed is that it is not the employment relationship that determines whether the child can stay in the institution, and that is why one can imagine, as I can otherwise hear, that some of the rapporteurs point out that one cannot imagine that both the children of a recipient of benefits and the children of a recipient of cash benefits can also go to an institution if it is either a former employee or it is one of the places which can then be filled by the municipality's instructions in accordance with general contractual principles and agreements. This means that we will not be able to set up an Arab-speaking institution, because the starting point in the future is that we speak Danish in Danish institutions, but that we also recognise that there are companies and there are municipalities where there is a need to focus on the fact that there can be both a recruitment need for international employees, but that there are also more and more Danish employees who are posted abroad for a shorter or longer period and therefore may have a wish to have an international offer to their children. These opportunities lie in the law today, but now we are making it clear that in future it is Danish that is the main language and that, if there are no integration problems associated with it, there may also be institutions in which English, German or French are the main language. This is an increase in the number of opportunities available today. However, I also think that both parts of the bill are a natural development of our day-offer structure, where we focus much more on what kind of issues are needed, what kind of things are demanded by families, what kind of flexibility that should also be in our day-offer area. I also think it is essential that we also listen politically, even though it may be a small legislative proposal, to the needs that may have landed over the development of opportunities in this area. That is why I would just like to say that I look forward to the further examination of the committee, including the fact that we can clarify the issues that have been raised today, but also the issues that may be further raised. I hope that this will lead to more parties ultimately backing the bill, because I can hear that perhaps there is something more mythical and mythical than perhaps the real expression of the draft law.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013173312	The debate is open, and the first to have access to the platform is Mr. Jan Johansen of the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013173333	The Danish society is characterised by a number of fundamental rights • the right to express oneself freely, the right to assemble and unite and the right to freely practise one's religion. But freedom is a duty, because with these rights the duty is also a duty to take care of the open, democratic and equal society that we have spent more than 100 years building. We must teach in our schools and in our educational institutions values such as freedom of expression, freedom of religion and equality and show a different path from the doctrinal religious path, and we must take action against those who oppose the democratic values of Danish society. In this context, it is vital that we stop public support or the setting up of premises for associations whose purpose and behaviour are to counter or undermine democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights. This is absolutely crucial if we are to put an end to the anti-democratic forces. It is against this background that we are today discussing a legislative proposal which translates part of the political agreement on initiatives aimed at religious preaching that was concluded at the end of the spring of this year. The agreement consists of a large number of sub-contracts and today we are discussing the implementation of the agreement which adjusts the National Education Act and thus the possibility for associations to obtain support and accommodation. It is, of course, very important to have the right balance in mind when we adjust legislation like this, but first and last we must prevent funding under the National Education Act from being given to associations that seek to work against or undermine the fundamental values of Denmark. The draft law therefore implements six initiatives to prevent support for such associations. First of all, the conditions for the public education associations are being tightened up in order to obtain support or to have premises assigned to them. This means that associations that oppose or undermine the basic values in Denmark cannot receive support or be assigned premises. Secondly, the municipality's obligation to supervise associations supported by the National Education Act is governed by law. Thirdly, the municipalities must create more transparency in relation to which associations receive support under the law. This applies to associations which receive support or are assigned premises, and to associations which are refused or associations which require the cessation or reimbursement of grants or the withdrawal of the right to use premises. Fourthly, the municipalities must be obliged to inform SKAT when the associations do not receive aid because of anti-democratic action. Fifthly, the ban on giving support to associations for worship or church activities is legalized. Sixthly, SKAT will have the possibility of informing other authorities of its own motion in relation to the re-evaluation of the possibility of granting a refusal and withdrawing a grant. This will in particular strengthen the cooperation between SKAT and the municipal councils. At the same time, it should be noted that an evaluation of the law is planned within two years of its entry into force. A legislative proposal such as this requires the right balance, so there is reason in the evaluation agreement. All in all, the draft law will contribute to the rejection by the municipality of associations which have anti-democratic purposes or behaviour that makes them unable to receive public support. It is so much the right way to go about getting rid of the forces that are working against the democratic values on which Denmark is founded. The bill is the implementation of a political agreement, and the Social Democrats support the bill.

This bill is another step on the road when it comes to combating Islamisation of Danish society, in addition to trying to limit the influx to Denmark, which is of course the most important thing, we also have to deal here at Christiansborg with the fact that Islam is increasingly dominant in various parts of society. And in our opinion, this is an expression of negative social development in the Danish People's Party, which must be taken seriously because we cannot escape the fact that there are elements in Islam that are incompatible on long distances with the value base on which Denmark is based and stands. It is a difficult exercise and there is no need to hide the fact that it is a difficult balancing exercise. Because if we are to be honest, it is very much about hitting those groups within the Muslim environments, which are, of course, so much involved in subversive work in society. That's kind of what this bill is about. In the Danish People's Party we are satisfied that it is clear from the draft law that the current legal situation is unacceptable. It should not be the case that an association can, on the one hand, work to abolish democracy in order to introduce a caliphate and, on the other hand, with municipal subsidies under the National Education Act, for example. organize football school for young people at the risk of the association's Islamist attitudes being passed on to the young people concerned. That is why, hopefully, a broad majority in the Danish Parliament wants us to change the Danish People's Party's Education Act, so that it will be clear from the legislation that people's enlightening activities, among other things. is based on democracy and freedoms, so that it is made a condition for the associations that they do not, by virtue of their purpose or by virtue of their behaviour, oppose or undermine democracy and a number of fundamental values: freedom of religion, freedom of expression, freedom of assembly and the fact that in Denmark we regard men and women as equally worthwhile. On the part of the Danish People's Party, we have emphasized that this time the Folketing has gone from word to action when it comes to fighting the extreme Muslims in society, and we think this bill is an expression of that. This is, of course, a difficult balancing exercise, but we think we are hitting it in a rather sensible way. Then there will be an evaluation in a few years' time, and then we will be able to look at what has happened. With these comments, I would like to say that the Danish People's Party can support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20161013190629	Unfortunately, the next debate cannot be so brief, and yet I think it is a good idea to take this a little more fully. Thank you to the Minister for Defence for allowing us to discuss the presentation of the Nordic defence and emergency cooperation.
