We fully agree with the Social Democratic Party to maintain and develop Nordic co-operation with the aim of creating a secure and secure Nordic region where we are ready for action against conflicts and disasters. The long-term goal is to work together at all levels where the Nordic countries have the same interests. I would therefore like to focus on individual and, in our opinion, the most important parts of the statement. Perhaps one can expect that the Danish Presidency of NORDEFCO, the Nordic Defence Association, can give an increased public interest in the work. We have a whole range of interesting tasks ahead of us. The so-called Easy Access agreement is probably the most important since it means that in peacetime the Nordic countries can travel anywhere in the agreement area with military capabilities. The secure communication agreement has already been implemented, while a common access agreement to radar information is under consideration. The agreement to grant unarmed military aircraft the right to apply to the nearest flight station will be extended to armed aircraft by the statements of Finland and Iceland. Cooperation with the Baltic States will become a growth point now that the three Baltic States participate in the Nordic ministerial and official meetings. The international aspect is touched upon in the report on UN peacekeeping in Mali, where three Nordic countries are part of a rotation scheme with Belgium and Portugal and where the starting point is the Norwegian camp. The overall Nordic effort in East Africa takes place within the framework of the reaction force EASF. They shall assist the African Union in its tasks. Continuing threats to security in East Africa make this effort particularly interesting. There are also a number of agreements which do not cover all the NORDEFCO countries, such as: the Danish-Swedish military agreement on increased access to each other's territories and with a number of related technical agreements. It is still under preparation. In the Social Democratic Party, we are of course interested in the Nordic Home Guard cooperation continuing to be inspiring and relevant, and the exchange of experiences between the Nordic countries is certainly of great importance. It is my impression that the volunteers in the Danish Home Guard benefit greatly from the cooperation, and we have now also opened the way for cooperation with the Home Guard in the Baltic, where the security policy challenges and the importance of the volunteers have been discussed. We also have much-developed cooperation in the field of emergency situations, where major accidents and disasters may occur with consequences for several countries. Here, too, the slogan is the robust society, which can at the same time prevent serious accidents and assist effectively if the accident is out. We also have cross-border cooperation in which we are available to third countries. We are working on the decisions on strategic development objectives that the participants are preparing for, for example. in the Haga cooperation. Other emergency cooperation covers all eventualities such as fire, crisis communication, etc., while Nordic rescue cooperation in NORDRED aims to assist neighbouring countries in preventing and reducing damage caused by accidents, disasters or human activity. Here too, existing agreements offer the possibility of developing cooperation. At an international level, the Nordic countries participate in disaster interventions in other parts of the world through IHP, which allows smaller countries such as Denmark to assist with disaster relief as needed. In particular, it is the United Nations which is the IHP's partner in the field of emergency assistance, such as: in the case of earthquakes, floods and other tasks. Our preparedness also includes a number of expert capabilities in areas such as nuclear radiation and high-risk chemical materials. Here a number of exercises are carried out jointly, as well as knowledge about hazardous materials, including in Greenland. As you know, we never got a Nordic defence union. On the other hand, we got something better: a long tradition of voluntary participation in defence and emergency cooperation. Our common area of work for the future is, firstly, to share tasks between the Nordic countries and, at the same time, to develop cooperation. Secondly, it would be desirable to cooperate on joint missions so that each country contributes what it is best to do. Thirdly, Nordic co-operation must consist more of joint efforts than at present, if current conditions are in favour of it, such as: an increased presence in the Baltic Sea region and the Baltic States. Fourthly, we must deal with the Arctic, and they must be constantly assessed so that the efforts of the Nordic countries to protect the Arctic areas are optimally addressed. A broad theme of Nordic co-operation is the fight against international terrorism, in which all aspects must be taken into account. We can only agree with the Minister that Nordic co-operation will strengthen our society's ability to offer citizens the greatest possible security on a common basis of trust and reciprocity. It must be used as a lever for our efforts at an internationally high level. I should like to thank the Minister for his statement, and then I should like to welcome the radical rapporteur and say that he agrees with these remarks.

On the alert side, the situation is formally different from that of the defence, as we have assumed responsibility for this area ourselves. We have a relatively well-run preparedness, which is led by the police on land and by the MRCC at sea. We have three ships and two flunching new rescue helicopters, which are paid by the Faroese Finance Act. As a fishing nation and with experience from nine oil wells on Faroese base, valuable experience has gradually been built up, although the matter should also be further developed. Here, too, Arctic Command is an important player, and the collaboration has developed positively over the years on the operational level. I myself was responsible for the helicopter section for many years and remember how difficult it could be to create open and effective cooperation, as it was difficult to open the military protocols. We could, for example. not land a civilian helicopter on a military ship, but today there is constructive operational cooperation with Arctic Command and, to that extent, also with Icelandic preparedness in the neighbouring country. But like the neighbours in Iceland and Greenland, we have a huge area to take care of in relation to our population, and therefore we must cooperate with our neighbours and provide support when the tasks require a greater readiness than what we can pattern, and vice versa, of course, we must also help with our neighbours. I also believe that there is a need for international cooperation on preparedness in the Arctic and neighbouring areas, so that resourceful economies that make use of the waters and thereby expose us to potential risks are also involved in the financing of a well-functioning preparedness. This could perhaps be inspired by ICAO cooperation in the field of aviation, where users pay a fee for the use of airspace, which is part of the financing of air traffic monitoring, communication and rescue. Since the Faroe Islands are not a full member of Nordic co-operation yet, but have full responsibility for preparedness in their own territory, a vacuum may arise in relation to communication and exchange of experience with other Nordic countries at a Nordic level. As I do not see the Faroe Islands mentioned in the statement at all, I would therefore like to ask the Minister whether the Faroe Islands have been dealt with in the Nordic emergency cooperation and whether the Minister will take note of the request that the Government respect the Faroe Islands' Home Rule Government as the supreme authority in the Faroe Islands, including with regard to defence policy initiatives.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013193831	Thank you, sir. Magni Arge. We move on to Mr. Sjúrður Skaale.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013193840	Thank you, sir. President. And also thank you for a fine statement. Personally, I think that all the Nordic countries should be members of both NATO and the European Union. Europe cannot be the two organisations apart. They create stability and security that everyone benefits from. Those who do not participate receive direct and indirect benefits, but they do not do anything. They're a kind of freeter, you might say. When you think of how strong the Nordic bloc could be both in the EU and in NATO, it is really a pity that we are not all involved. The North would be much stronger together. The sum of our weight would be far greater if we were together than it is when we are split. And it is going to be a double shame when you consider that part of the problem arises because of purely identity policy. That is, for example, the case. It is difficult to see anything other than the reason why Norway is outside the EU; it is difficult to see any logical argument for it. On the other hand, it is understandable that Finland will not be part of NATO; that is logical because of their neighbour. And you also understand that Iceland will not be part of the European Union. And for the same reason that EU membership would be complicated for Iceland, it could also be complicated for the Faroe Islands at least as long as the fisheries policy is as it is. So that is the way it is, and as long as it is so, it will be difficult to have a really close defence cooperation over. But it's good, and it's necessary to try especially when you have a potentially aggressive neighbor in the east. The statement which forms the basis of this debate shows that the opportunities are being cultivated. But I'm a little puzzled, as Mr. Magni Arge also mentioned that the Faroe Islands are not even mentioned in the statement. The Danish Presidency has worked for the so-called easy access agreement to ensure that countries can have access to each other's air and sea areas in a flexible way. I assume that this includes Faroese areas as well. This is clearly in the interests of the Faroe Islands, but it would be interesting to know how and whether the Faroe Islands have been involved in those discussions. There is no doubt that the geopolitical importance of the Faroe Islands, which was quite great during the Cold War, has grown in the new security policy situation, which we have now also seen as a result of the potentially aggressive neighbour to the east. The last thing that Magni Arge also mentioned is the stream of visits that have been to the Faroe Islands in the last year. In June, the U.S. Secretary of the Navy, the U.S. Navy Minister, was in the Faroe Islands. Several defence-related delegations have been there, and last year there was a British frigate in the Faroe Islands for the first time since the Cold War ended. And the Taksøe report also very clearly indicated that the Danish Government's security and defence policy look turns more northwards. I appreciate the investments in security that the government intends to make in the Faroe Islands, not least the fact that a frigate will patrol regularly around the islands as intended in the future. In the defence settlement, it is also said that a new radar station might come up instead of the one that was shut down after the cold war. I personally welcome this too, but I would like to remind you, as I have done before, that everything that is happening around and on the Faroe Islands in this context must be done in a transparent manner with the Faroese authorities and in dialogue with the Faroese authorities. We must avoid the myth formation which was so extensive a few decades back and which always occurs when things happen over people's heads. I do not see that Denmark and the Faroe Islands have different interests in terms of security and defence, and that is why we must avoid going wrong with each other. I don't think that's necessary. We have, as has also been mentioned, a 2005 declaration on involvement in security and defence and foreign affairs, the Fámjin Declaration, and if it is followed, I believe that we can have good open-minded and mythless cooperation in the future. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20161013194316	Thank you to the rapporteur. No more rapporteurs have asked to speak, so that is the Minister for Defence.
